=== PROMPT VARIANT ===

=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
In the nation of AtCoder, there are N cities numbered 1 to N, and M roads numbered 1 to M.
Road i connects cities A_i and B_i bidirectionally and has a length of C_i.
For each i = 1, \ldots, M, determine whether the following two values are different.

- The shortest distance from city 1 to city N when all roads are passable
- The shortest distance from city 1 to city N when the M - 1 roads other than road i are passable

If city N can be reached from city 1 in one of these cases but not the other, the two values are considered different.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
N M
A_1 B_1 C_1
\vdots
A_M B_M C_M

Output

Print M lines. The i-th line should contain Yes if the shortest distance from city 1 to city N when all roads are passable is different from the shortest distance when the M - 1 roads other than road i are passable, and No otherwise.
If city N can be reached from city 1 in one of these cases but not the other, the two values are considered different.

Constraints


- 2 \leq N \leq 2 \times 10^5
- 1 \leq M \leq 2 \times 10^5
- 1 \leq A_i < B_i \leq N
- All pairs (A_i, B_i) are distinct.
- 1 \leq C_i \leq 10^9
- City N can be reached from city 1 when all roads are passable.
- All input values are integers.

Sample Input 1

3 3
1 2 5
1 3 10
2 3 6

Sample Output 1

No
Yes
No

When all roads are passable, the shortest distance from city 1 to city 3 is 10.

- When the two roads other than road 1 are passable, the shortest distance is 10.
- When the two roads other than road 2 are passable, the shortest distance is 11.
- When the two roads other than road 3 are passable, the shortest distance is 10.

Sample Input 2

4 6
2 3 1
2 4 1
3 4 1
1 2 1
1 3 1
1 4 1

Sample Output 2

No
No
No
No
No
Yes

When all roads are passable, the shortest distance from city 1 to city 4 is 1.
When the five roads other than road 6 are passable, the shortest distance is 2.

Sample Input 3

2 1
1 2 1

Sample Output 3

Yes

When the zero roads other than road 1 are passable, city 2 cannot be reached from city 1.

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Thus answer for each original road i:
- Build H directed edges as above.
- Compute dominators of t in H (from source 1). Need know for each vertex whether it dominates t. Actually only need dominator set of t. In a DAG, can compute dominators of t maybe via reverse graph? But we can compute all dominators of t using dominator tree or simpler because H is DAG and maybe all vertices on some shortest path. Need efficient O((N+M) log N) or O(N+M). Since H is DAG, dominators can be computed by DP in topological order:

Implement LCA function:
def lca(a,b):
    if depth[a] < depth[b]: swap
    # lift a to depth b
    diff = depth[a]-depth[b]
    bit=0
    while diff:
       if diff&1: a=up[bit][a]
       diff >>=1; bit+=1
    if a==b: return a
    for k in reversed(range(LOG)):
       if up[k][a] != up[k][b]:
           a=up[k][a]; b=up[k][b]
    return up[0][a]
Need up[k][root]=root, depth[root]=0.

When adding new vertex v with parent p:
depth[v] = depth[p] + 1
up[0][v] = p
for k in 1..LOG-1: up[k][v] = up[k-1][ up[k-1][v] ]
Need ensure up[k-1][v] already computed; yes.

What about graph where preds have LCA that is not a dominator of v? Suppose preds p,q, LCA r. Is r guaranteed to dominate v? Since every s-v path must enter v through one of preds. Any path to a pred p goes through all ancestors of p, including r. Thus any path to v goes through r. So r dominates v. And any common dominator of all preds is an ancestor of all preds, hence ancestor of LCA? Actually LCA is deepest common ancestor; any common ancestor is ancestor of LCA. Thus idom(v)=LCA. Good.

Now, need compute LCA of multiple predecessors efficiently. We can do:
l = -1
for p in rev[v]:
    if l == -1: l = p
    else: l = lca(l, p)
idom[v] = l
This is O(total predecessors * LOG) = O(M log N). M 2e5, LOG 19, okay.

Implementation plan:

1. Read N,M.
2. Initialize adj = [[] for _ in range(N)]
   arrays A=[0]*M, B=[0]*M, C=[0]*M
   For i in range(M):
      a,b,c = map(int, input().split()); a-=1; b-=1
      A[i]=a; B[i]=b; C[i]=c
      adj[a].append((b,c)); adj[b].append((a,c))
3. Dijkstra:
   def dijkstra(start):
       dist=[INF]*N
       dist[start]=0
       pq=[(0,start)]
       while pq:
          d,v=heappop(pq)
          if d!=dist[v]: continue
          for to,w in adj[v]:
             nd=d+w
             if nd<dist[to]:
                dist[to]=nd; heappush(pq,(nd,to))
       return dist
   dist1 = dijkstra(0)
   distN = dijkstra(N-1)
   D = dist1[N-1]
4. Build H:
   h_vertices = []
   for v in range(N):
       if dist1[v] + distN[v] == D:
           h_vertices.append(v)
   h_rev = [[] for _ in range(N)]
   h_edges = []  # list of (u,v,idx)
   indeg = [0]*N
   for i in range(M):
       a=A[i]; b=B[i]; c=C[i]
       if dist1[a] + c + distN[b] == D:
           h_rev[b].append(a)
           indeg[b] += 1
           h_edges.append((a,b,i))
       elif dist1[b] + c + distN[a] == D:
           h_rev[a].append(b)
           indeg[a] += 1
           h_edges.append((b,a,i))
   # Note: if both conditions true? impossible, but elif okay. If both true due to some bug, would add only one; but impossible.
5. Sort h_vertices by dist1:
   h_vertices.sort(key=lambda x: dist1[x])
   # Could include source? yes.
6. Dominator tree via LCA:
   LOG = (N+1).bit_length()
   up = [[0]*N for _ in range(LOG)]  # maybe memory; or use arrays.
   depth = [0]*N
   idom = [-1]*N
   # We need process h_vertices. But up table for vertices not in H remains 0. LCA only called on H vertices.
   for v in h_vertices:
       if v == 0:  # source city 1
           idom[v] = v
           depth[v] = 0
           up[0][v] = v
           for k in range(1, LOG):
               up[k][v] = v
       else:
           # compute lca of h_rev[v]
           l = -1
           for p in h_rev[v]:
               if l == -1:
                   l = p
               else:
                   l = lca(l, p)
           # l should not be -1
           idom[v] = l
           depth[v] = depth[l] + 1
           up[0][v] = l
           for k in range(1, LOG):
               up[k][v] = up[k-1][ up[k-1][v] ]
   Need define lca before loop. It uses up and depth. But lca function can be nested and access up/depth.
   lca implementation:
       def lca(a,b):
           if depth[a] < depth[b]:
               a,b = b,a
           diff = depth[a] - depth[b]
           bit = 0
           while diff:
               if diff & 1:
                   a = up[bit][a]
               diff >>= 1
               bit += 1
           if a == b:
               return a
           for k in range(LOG-1, -1, -1):
               if up[k][a] != up[k][b]:
                   a = up[k][a]
                   b = up[k][b]
           return up[0][a]
   Note: If a or b is 0? We never call with 0. Root parent root. Good.
7. Mark dominators of t:
   is_dom = [False]*N
   v = N-1
   while True:
       is_dom[v] = True
       if v == 0:
           break
       v = idom[v]
   # idom[t] should be set. If t not in h_vertices? impossible.
8. Answer:
   ans = ["No"]*M
   for u,v,idx in h_edges:
       if is_dom[v] and indeg[v] == 1:
           ans[idx] = "Yes"
   print("\n".join(ans))

Need be careful: If indeg[v]==1, h_edges may contain exactly one edge to v. But what if h_edges contains an edge to v that is not the unique incoming because there is another incoming edge from a road that was not added due to both conditions? No. If indeg[v]==1, unique. Good.

But wait: What if head v dominates t and indeg[v]==1, but the unique incoming edge e is not in h_edges? Impossible because indeg counts h_edges.

Now, let's test with a graph where head dominates t but indeg=1, but the edge's tail is not the immediate dominator? If indeg=1, tail is the only predecessor, so tail dominates head and idom(head)=tail. Good.

Now, let's think about if we can optimize Dijkstra by using `heapq` and local variables. Code:
def dijkstra(start):
    dist = [INF] * N
    dist[start] = 0
    pq = [(0, start)]
    while pq:
        d, v = heappop(pq)
        if d != dist[v]:
            continue
        for to, w in adj[v]:
            nd = d + w
            if nd < dist[to]:
                dist[to] = nd
                heappush(pq, (nd, to))
    return dist

Add: dist1[a]+distN[a]+dist1[b]+distN[b]+2c=2D.
But dist1[a]+distN[a] >= D and dist1[b]+distN[b] >= D, so LHS >= 2D+2c > 2D. Contradiction. Good.
